13

14

15

17

18

19

Q. 
Please explain which data sets were updated and why the Company 
7 
considered it important to make the updates listed above?  
8 
A. 
The specific data sets updated include: 
9 
• CSS billing system historical energy deliveries and customer 
10 
counts data; 
11 
• Hourly meter data services peak data; 
12 
• Economic and pricing actuals and projections; 
13 
• Weather variable actuals and projections; 
14 
• DER actuals and projections;  
15 
• EV energy impact profile; and 
 
16 
• Inclusion of electric heat pumps as a new DER. 
17 
The Company considered it important to update the datasets for two 
18 
reasons.  One, to include the three additional months of input data not 
19 
previously available that includes the “re-opening” phases in upstate New 
20 


--- Page 8 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 7 of 19 
 
York as related to the COVID-19 pandemic.  Two, to incorporate the most 
1 
recent summer peak season into the dataset that is used to inform future 
2 
projections for peak loads and which also includes the adjustment for the 
3 
impact of the pandemic on this past summer’s peak.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please explain why models were re-specified for your corrections and 
6 
updates testimony and discuss any models with significant changes to 
7 
the dependent variables included or not included.  
8 
A. 
All retail energy deliveries, retail customer counts, and peak models were 
9 
re-specified because three months of additional input data (June, July, and 
10 
August) were included.  When models are re-specified, even if all of the 
11 
same dependent variables are included, there will always be some minor 
12 
changes to the coefficients and model statistics and thus resulting model 
13 
results.   Exhibits __ (JFG-30CU) and Exhibit __ (JFG-31CU) show the 
14 
details of the updated models. 
15 
 
16 
 
During this process, no significant variables were changed for any of the 
17 
retail energy deliveries or the wholesale energy models.  There were 
18 
however, several changes to the customer count models and the peak 
19 
forecast model. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 9 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 8 of 19 
 
 
For the residential customer counts model, the input data set was increased 
1 
to include historical years starting in 2004, compared to the original 
2 
starting point of 2015.  The general approach was to use more of the 
3 
original data set in terms of years available than less.  No changes were 
4 
made to the use of the trend variable in the model.  Overall model 
5 
performance, as measured by the R-squared test, remained excellent at 
6 
0.99, which matches that of the original July filing.  Regarding the 
7 
statistical strength of the trend variable as measured by the p-value 
8 
statistic, it also continued to be very significant at <0.001, as it was in the 
9 
July filing.  The practical impact of this change, as well as the inclusion of 
10 
additional three months of actual historical data, is that the Rate Year 
11 
residential customer count is projected at 1,520,730 instead of 1,525,345 
12 
in the July filing.  
13 
 
14 
 
 For the commercial customer counts model, the input data set was 
15 
increased to include years starting in 2005 versus the original starting 
16 
point of 2015.  The key economic variable was changed to per capita 
17 
income and a time trend versus the number of households in the July 
18 
filing.  Overall model performance, as measured by the R-squared 
19 
statistical measure remained excellent at 0.99 versus the same value in the 
20 
July filing.  The p-values on both the trend variable (at <0.0001) and the 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 9 of 19 
 
pci variable (at 0.058) are significant.  The practical impact of these 
1 
changes is to raise the annual two-year growth rate (to the Rate Year 
2 
2022) to negative 0.1 percent per year and the five-year growth rate to 0.2 
3 
percent per year versus the negative -1.4 percent (two-year) and -0.4 
4 
percent (five year) in the Company’s July filing.  Historical growth rates 
5 
have been 1.0 percent per year (five-year average) and 0.6 percent per year 
6 
(ten-year average) so a positive growth over the next five years is more 
7 
consistent with history.  In the Moody’s May 2020 economic forecast 
8 
release, which was used in the Company’s July forecast, the number of 
9 
households was expected to decrease starting in May 2020, and continue 
10 
decreasing through late 2022.  Over the past three months (June to August 
11 
2020), however, the Company’s customer counts did not decrease.  This, 
12 
along with the future long-term negative growth rate produced using 
13 
households mentioned above, indicates the number of households is no 
14 
longer a suitable variable to serve as the economic indicator for the 
15 
customer counts forecast.  
16 
 
17 
 
For the industrial customer counts model, manufacturing employment was 
18 
added as a second variable to the existing time trend only variable used in 
19 
the Company’s July filing.  The preference is to use economic variables 
20 
instead of simply time trends when statistically significant to the model.  
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 10 of 19 
 
This helps to capture changes in growth due to changes in the economy. 
1 
Overall model performance, as measured by the R-squared statistical 
2 
measure remained excellent at 0.99, which matches that of the July filing.  
3 
Regarding the statistical strength of the dependent variables themselves, 
4 
the p-values of both the manufacturing employment and the time-trend 
5 
variables are excellent at <0.0001.  The practical impact of this change is 
6 
that the Rate Year industrial customer count is projected at 1,501 instead 
7 
of 1,504 in the July filing.  It is noted that, because the industrial energy 
8 
deliveries model is a direct kwh model and not an energy use per customer 
9 
model, the output of this customer counts model does not impact the 
10 
energy deliveries model.  
11 
 
12 
 
Peak model:  In the model included in the Company’s July filing, gigawatt 
13 
hours (“GWh”) and number of households were used as the primary 
14 
variables driving growth.  In the updated forecast number of households 
15 
was replaced with a time trend.  GWh remains in the model.  Overall 
16 
model performance, as measured by the R-squared statistical measure, 
17 
improved to 0.977 compared to 0.937 in the July filing.  The p-values on 
18 
the GWh and time trend variables are significant.  The practical impact of 
19 
these changes is little change to the annual one- and two-year future peak 
20 
projections (which span the Rate Year) in this updated forecast versus the 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 11 of 19 
 
July forecast.  The new year 2021 and year 2022 peak projections (50/50, 
1 
net) are 6,639 megawatts (“MW”) and 6,603 MW, respectively, compared 
2 
to 6,651 MW (2021) and 6,606 MW (2022) in the July filing.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please explain why the weather-normal values in the peak forecast 
5 
model were updated and why the Company thought it important to 
6 
make these updates.  
7 
A. 
The weather normal (50/50) and extremes (90/10 and 95/5) are updated 
8 
each year with the latest 20-years of history.  Since the Company now has 
9 
summer season 2020, the weather values are updated with the years 2001 
10 
to 2020 where in the July filing the timeframe used was years 2000 to 
11 
2019.  The new weighted temperature-humidity indexes (WTHIs) are now 
12 
80.7 (50/50), 82.2 (90/10), and 82.6 (95/5) versus 80.6, 82.1, and 82.5 in 
13 
the July filing.  Exhibit __ (JFG-20CU) shows additional detail.  
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please explain why the process for weather-adjusting the 2020 
16 
summer peak values was altered from that used in previous years.  
17 
A. 
During the summer of 2020, the region was in the midst of the pandemic 
18 
and in the process of re-opening various activities and businesses across 
19 
the summer months.  The timing of the peak day was greatly influenced by 
20 
this activity.  During the earlier part of the summer, weather adjusted loads 
21 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 12 of 19 
 
were lower in general than they were later in the summer as the economy 
1 
re-opened.  Therefore, in addition to the standard WTHI weather variable 
2 
used in prior years, a weekly trend variable was added to the process.  This 
3 
trend was used to approximate these additional loads coming back on-line 
4 
as the summer progressed.  This is similar to the process the NYISO is 
5 
using for this summer.  The summer is considered June through August.  
6 
Week one of the summer period is the first week of June, and the last full 
7 
week of August was week thirteen.  The Company’s summer peak was on 
8 
July 9 (hour-ending 18).  The week of July 9 was week number six, less 
9 
than half of the way through the full summer season.  The value 
10 
(coefficient) of this weekly trend variable was 36.8 and was significant at 
11 
<0.001, as measured by the p-value.  The overall explanatory power of the 
12 
model, as measured by the R-squared statistic, is good at 0.91.  The 
13 
practical impact of using this approach was to add 257.6 MW ((13-6) * 
14 
36.8) to the weather-only adjusted summer peak.  No other changes were 
15 
made to the process from that explained in the direct testimony.  
16 
Exhibit__(JFG-31CU) contains a summary of the model and its associated 
17 
statistics. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Did the Company make any explicit adjustments in the forecasts for 
20 
the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic?  
21 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 13 of 19 
 
A. 
For the retail energy deliveries and customer count forecasts, no out-of-
1 
model adjustments were required.  The use of the historical data sets 
2 
through August, which contains six months of pandemic data, and the 
3 
latest Moody’s economic forecast, which accounts for the economic 
4 
impacts of the pandemic, capture the pandemic impacts for the forecast by 
5 
using the most recent data available.  
6 
 
7 
 
Similarly, in the peak model, no out-of-model adjustments were necessary 
8 
to capture the pandemic impact going forward because the Moody’s 
9 
forecast accounts for the pandemic impact on the economy.  As discussed 
10 
above, however, the model used for the weather adjustment for the 2020 
11 
peak reflects how the load recovered from the initial pandemic impact by 
12 
the end of the summer.  
13 
 
 
14 
Q. 
Please explain what changes to the projections for number of vehicles 
15 
were made and the reasoning for making those changes.  
16 
A. 
The projected vehicle count for electric vehicles in the July filing was 
17 
based on a continuing trend in the number of new vehicles based on the 
18 
historical data set.  This did not account for possible impacts of the Make-
19 
Ready Program Order issued on July 16, 2020.  The Make-Ready Program 
20 
Order allows additional spending to accelerate installations of charging 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 14 of 19 
 
stations in the service territory.  While the overarching concepts of the 
1 
Make-Ready Program Order are to move more quickly towards a zero 
2 
emissions construct, no specific vehicle target goals were established.  
3 
Nevertheless, the Company re-visited its assumptions for the number of 
4 
projected electric vehicles during the periods relevant to this rate filing, 
5 
including the two additional Data Years and two additional informational 
6 
years.  The Company is now basing its electric vehicle projections on 
7 
Bloomberg’s fifth annual Long-term Electric Vehicle Outlook (“BNEF”), 
8 
dated May 18, 2020.  It is a well-known and comprehensive study of the 
9 
electric vehicle sector, where technology, policy, and economic factors 
10 
that drive the electric vehicle market were thoroughly studied.  
11 
Considering the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, BNEF expects the 
12 
electric vehicle sales to be relatively modest through 2025.  It predicts a 
13 
transformational technology break-through for electric vehicle adoption in 
14 
the second half of this decade.  The Company’s new EV forecast is now 
15 
based on moving from the currently installed number of EVs towards the 
16 
Bloomberg longer-term forecast.  Specifically, annual growth in electric 
17 
vehicles over the two-year period between now and the Rate Year 2022 is 
18 
15.6 percent versus 23.9 percent in the prior forecast and 21.8 percent 
19 
versus 20.8 percent over the five-year period.  The significant decrease in 
20 
the two-year growth rate was mainly driven by the lower expectation 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 15 of 19 
 
BNEF has for EV sales due to the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic.  In 
1 
the longer-term (i.e., five years), the growth rates are close.  Exhibit __ 
2 
(JFG-21CU) shows the revised EV projections.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please explain what changes were made to the energy and peak per 
5 
vehicle impacts and the reasoning for making those changes.  
6 
A. 
The per vehicle kWh impact used to determine retail energy deliveries in 
7 
the July filing was 3,594 kWh per vehicle and was left fixed at that value 
8 
for the duration of the Rate Year and Data Years, as well as the additional 
9 
informational years (2024 to 2025).  This assumes that the mix of battery 
10 
only and hybrid vehicles remain the same.  Under the original vehicle 
11 
count projections based on the trend in the July filing, this was a 
12 
reasonable assumption in the near-term.  Under the new vehicle count 
13 
approach discussed above, with more accelerated vehicle adoption, this 
14 
assumption was re-visited.  It is now assumed that there will be a slowly 
15 
changing mix of types of vehicles from hybrids to more battery-only 
16 
vehicles over time.  As this takes place, there will be more electric 
17 
charging use over time.  The assumption used is that the battery-only 
18 
electric vehicle percentage will grow from 32 percent as of 2019 to close 
19 
to 70 percent by year 2030.  Thus, for this filing, the fixed kwh value of 
20 
3,594, kwh is assumed to grow at a linear value of 25.35 more kwh per 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 16 of 19 
 
year.  The impacts on the peak forecast remain the same as the July filing, 
1 
and the Company plans to further study it in the future.  Exhibit __ (JFG-
2 
26CU) shows the new annual values for energy.  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please explain why electric heat pumps were added as an additional 
5 
DER, how the number of installations was determined, and what their 
6 
impacts are to the retail energy deliveries and peak forecasts.  
7 
A. 
Electric heat pumps were added as an additional DER because they are a 
8 
significant contributor to the state policy goals for beneficial 
9 
electrification (electric vehicles being the other).  At the time of the July 
10 
filing, the Commission had recently issued the New Efficiency New York 
11 
(NE:NY) Order, which had targets for both energy efficiency and electric 
12 
heat pumps in it.   As discussed in the July filing, the Company recognized 
13 
this, but the specifics of the electric heat pump impacts were still being 
14 
evaluated.  The Company now has these impacts and thus is adding heat 
15 
pump impacts to the forecast.  
16 
 
17 
The number of installations of heat pumps through year 2025 is per the 
18 
NE:NY Order.  For year 2026, a continuation of the trend through year 
19 
2025 is made.  The per unit impact to the retail energy deliveries forecast 
20 
is determined using information from NYSERDA’s report on heat 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Joseph F. Gredder, Electric Sales Forecast 
 
 
Page 17 of 19 
 
pumps.1  The Company is not making any assumptions for electric heat 
1 
pump reductions to the peak forecast (which occurs in the summer) 
2 
because the primary impacts for these units would be during the heating 
3 
season.  Although there may be a small savings associated with the 
4 
cooling efficiencies of the electric heat pumps, some of these savings may 
5 
already be captured as part of the energy efficiency programs that 
6 
incentivize cooling efficiencies.  It is noted that the ISO-NE similarly is 
7 
not making any assumptions for summer peak savings and the NYISO is 
8 
actually showing small increases.  The Company will monitor the types of 
9 
heat pump systems installed and may update this assumption in the future.  
10 
Exhibit __ (JFG-29CU) contains the impacts and additional details.  
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the overall changes to the Company’s electric retail 
13 
energy deliveries forecasts. 
14 
A. 
The total retail energy deliveries (net) projected for the Rate Year 2022 are 
15 
32,019 GWh now versus 31,605 GWh in the July filing (+ 1.3 percent).  
16 
By revenue class for the Rate Year the values are: 
17 
• Residential:  11,488 GWh now versus 11,422 GWh in my direct 
18 
testimony (+ 0.6 percent).   
19 
                                                 
1 NYSERDA Report, “New Efficiency: New York, Analysis of Residential Heat Pumps 
Potential and Economics,” dated January 2019.

Page 18 of 19 
 
• Commercial:  11,434 GWh now versus 11,324 GWh in my direct 
1 
testimony (+ 1.0 percent).   
2 
• Industrial:  9,097 GWh now versus 8,859 GWh in my direct testimony 
3 
(+/ 2.7 percent).   
4 
 
5 
For the peak forecasts the projected 50/50 net peak for the next two 
6 
summers are:   
7 
• Calendar year 2021 is 6,639 MW versus 6,651 MW (-0.2 percent) in 
8 
the July filing.   
9 
• Calendar year 2022 is 6,603 MW versus 6,606 MW (-0.06 percent).   
10 
 
11 
 
The revised exhibits to my testimony contain a complete update to all 
12 
previously filed exhibits, showing additional detail on all retail energy 
13 
deliveries, customer counts and peak forecast values.  
14 
 
15

16 
38CU) as related to the retail energy deliveries and customer counts 
17 
forecast and Exhibits __(JFG-39CU) and Exhibit __ (JFG-40CU) as 
18 
related to the peak forecast were added.

through August 2020 and updated projected data for the explanatory
